The ad [alludes](/wiki/Alludes "Alludes") to [George Orwell](/wiki/George_Orwell "George Orwell")'s novel _[Nineteen Eighty-Four](/wiki/Nineteen_Eighty-Four "Nineteen Eighty-Four")_ , which describes a [dystopian future](/wiki/Dystopian_future "Dystopian future") ruled by a televised "[Big Brother](/wiki/Big_Brother_\(1984\) "Big Brother \(1984\)")".[269][270]

The Macintosh, however, was expensive, which hindered its ability to be competitive in a market already dominated by the [Commodore 64](/wiki/Commodore_64 "Commodore 64") for consumers, and the [IBM Personal Computer](/wiki/IBM_Personal_Computer "IBM Personal Computer") and its accompanying [clone](/wiki/IBM_PC_compatible "IBM PC compatible") market for businesses.[271] Macintosh systems still found success in education and desktop publishing and kept Apple as the second-largest PC manufacturer for the next decade. 

### NeXT Computer

Main article: [NeXT Computer](/wiki/NeXT_Computer "NeXT Computer")

After Jobs was forced out of Apple in 1985, he started [NeXT](/wiki/NeXT "NeXT"), a [workstation](/wiki/Workstation "Workstation") computer company. The NeXT Computer was introduced in 1988 at a [lavish launch event](/wiki/NeXT_Introduction "NeXT Introduction"). Using the NeXT Computer, [Tim Berners-Lee](/wiki/Tim_Berners-Lee "Tim Berners-Lee") created the world's first [web browser](/wiki/Web_browser "Web browser"), the [WorldWideWeb](/wiki/WorldWideWeb "WorldWideWeb"). The NeXT Computer's operating system, named [NeXTSTEP](/wiki/NeXTSTEP "NeXTSTEP"), begat [Darwin](/wiki/Darwin_\(operating_system\) "Darwin \(operating system\)"), which is now the foundation of most of Apple's [operating systems](/wiki/Operating_system "Operating system") such as Macintosh's [macOS](/wiki/MacOS "MacOS") and iPhone's [iOS](/wiki/IOS "IOS").[272][273]

### iMac

Main article: [iMac](/wiki/IMac "IMac")

[](/wiki/File:IMac_Bondi_Blue.jpg)The original [iMac](/wiki/IMac_G3 "IMac G3") was introduced in 1998 as the first consumer-facing Apple product to have debuted after Jobs's return.

Apple's [iMac G3](/wiki/IMac_G3 "IMac G3") was introduced in 1998 and its innovative design is directly the result of Jobs's return to Apple. Apple boasted "the back of our computer looks better than the front of anyone else's".[274] Described as "cartoonlike", the first iMac, clad in Bondi Blue plastic, was unlike any personal computer that came before. In 1999, Apple introduced the Graphite gray Apple iMac and since has varied the shape, color and size considerably while maintaining the [all-in-one](/wiki/All-in-one_PC "All-in-one PC") design. Design ideas were intended to create a connection with the user such as the handle and a "breathing" light effect when the computer went to sleep.[275] The Apple iMac sold for $1,299 at that time. The iMac's forward-thinking changes include eschewing the [floppy disk drive](/wiki/Floppy_disk_drive "Floppy disk drive") and moving exclusively to [USB](/wiki/USB "USB") for connecting peripherals. Through the iMac's success, USB was popularized among third-party peripheral makers—as evidenced by the fact that many early USB peripherals were made of translucent plastic to match the iMac design.[276]

### iTunes

Main article: [iTunes](/wiki/ITunes "ITunes")

iTunes is a [media player](/wiki/Media_player_software "Media player software"), media library, online radio broadcaster, and mobile device management application developed by Apple. It is used to play, download, and organize digital [audio](/wiki/Sound_recording_and_reproduction "Sound recording and reproduction") and video on personal computers running the [macOS](/wiki/MacOS "MacOS") and [Microsoft Windows](/wiki/Microsoft_Windows "Microsoft Windows") operating systems. The [iTunes Store](/wiki/ITunes_Store "ITunes Store") is also available on the [iPod Touch](/wiki/IPod_Touch "IPod Touch"), iPhone, and iPad.[277]

Through the iTunes Store, users can purchase and download music, music videos, television shows, [audiobooks](/wiki/Audiobook "Audiobook"), [podcasts](/wiki/Podcast "Podcast"), movies, and movie rentals in some countries, and [ringtones](/wiki/Ringtone "Ringtone"), available on the iPhone and iPod Touch (fourth generation onward). [Application software](/wiki/Application_software "Application software") for the iPhone, iPad and iPod Touch can be downloaded from the [App Store](/wiki/App_Store_\(iOS\) "App Store \(iOS\)").[277]

### iPod

Main article: [iPod](/wiki/IPod "IPod")

The [first generation of iPod](/wiki/IPod_Classic#1st_generation "IPod Classic") was released October 23, 2001. The major innovation of the iPod was its small size achieved by using a 1.8" hard drive compared to the 2.5" drives common to players at that time. The capacity of the first-generation iPod ranged from 5 GB to 10 GB.[278] The iPod sold for US$399 and more than 100,000 iPods were sold before the end of 2001. The introduction of the iPod resulted in Apple becoming a major player in the music industry.[279] Also, the iPod's success prepared the way for the iTunes music store and the iPhone.[280] After the first few generations of iPod, Apple released the touchscreen [iPod Touch](/wiki/IPod_Touch "IPod Touch"), the reduced-size [iPod Mini](/wiki/IPod_Mini "IPod Mini") and [iPod Nano](/wiki/IPod_Nano "IPod Nano"), and the screenless [iPod Shuffle](/wiki/IPod_Shuffle "IPod Shuffle") in the following years.[279]

### iPhone

Main article: [iPhone](/wiki/IPhone "IPhone")

Apple began work on the [first iPhone](/wiki/IPhone_\(1st_generation\) "IPhone \(1st generation\)") in 2005 and the first iPhone was released on June 29, 2007. The iPhone created such a sensation that a survey indicated six out of ten Americans were aware of its release. _[Time](/wiki/Time_\(magazine\) "Time \(magazine\)")_ declared it "Invention of the Year" for 2007 and included it in the All-TIME 100 Gadgets list in 2010, in the category of Communication.[281] The completed iPhone had multimedia capabilities and functioned as a quad-band touch screen smartphone. A year later, the [iPhone 3G](/wiki/IPhone_3G "IPhone 3G") was released in July 2008 with three key features: support for GPS, 3G data and tri-band UMTS/HSDPA. In June 2009, the [iPhone 3GS](/wiki/IPhone_3GS "IPhone 3GS"), whose improvements included voice control, a better camera, and a faster processor, was introduced by Phil Schiller.[282] The iPhone 4 was thinner than previous models, had a five megapixel camera capable of recording video in 720p HD, and added a secondary front-facing camera for video calls.[283] A major feature of the [iPhone 4s](/wiki/IPhone_4s "IPhone 4s"), introduced in October 2011, was [Siri](/wiki/Siri "Siri"), a virtual assistant capable of voice recognition.[284]

### iPad

Main article: [iPad](/wiki/IPad "IPad")

[](/wiki/File:Steve_Jobs_with_the_Apple_iPad_no_logo.jpg)Jobs introduced the [iPad](/wiki/IPad "IPad") in 2010.

The iPad is an iOS-based line of [tablet computers](/wiki/Tablet_computer "Tablet computer") designed and marketed by Apple. The [first iPad](/wiki/First_iPad "First iPad") was released on April 3, 2010. The [user interface](/wiki/User_interface "User interface") is built around the device's [multi-touch](/wiki/Multi-touch "Multi-touch") screen, including a [virtual keyboard](/wiki/Virtual_keyboard "Virtual keyboard"). The iPad includes built-in [Wi-Fi](/wiki/Wi-Fi "Wi-Fi") and cellular connectivity on select models. As of April 2015[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Steve_Jobs&action=edit), more than 250 million iPads have been sold.[285]

## Personal life

[](/wiki/File:SteveJobs_house_in_PaloAlto_with_fruit_trees.jpg)Jobs's house in Palo Alto

### Habits and public image

Jobs was known to frequently appear [barefoot](/wiki/Barefoot "Barefoot") in public, a habit that stemmed from his countercultural background, and was depicted in the movies _[Pirates of Silicon Valley](/wiki/Pirates_of_Silicon_Valley "Pirates of Silicon Valley")_[286] and _[Jobs](/wiki/Jobs_\(film\) "Jobs \(film\)")_.[287] It also had an impact on American corporate culture,[288] and was adopted by some other entrepreneurs, most notably [Adam Neumann](/wiki/Adam_Neumann "Adam Neumann").[289]

### Marriage

In 1989, Jobs first met his future wife, [Laurene Powell](/wiki/Laurene_Powell "Laurene Powell"), when he gave a lecture at the [Stanford Graduate School of Business](/wiki/Stanford_Graduate_School_of_Business "Stanford Graduate School of Business"), where she was a student. Soon after the event, he stated that Laurene "was right there in the front row in the lecture hall, and I couldn't take my eyes off of her ... kept losing my train of thought, and started feeling a little giddy".[290] After the lecture, he met her in the parking lot and invited her out to dinner. From that point forward, they were together, with a few minor exceptions, for the rest of his life.[291]

Jobs proposed on New Year's Day 1990; they married on March 18, 1991, in a Buddhist ceremony at the [Ahwahnee Hotel](/wiki/Ahwahnee_Hotel "Ahwahnee Hotel") in [Yosemite National Park](/wiki/Yosemite_National_Park "Yosemite National Park").[292] Fifty people, including Jobs's father, Paul, and his sister Mona, attended. The ceremony was conducted by Jobs's [guru](/wiki/Guru "Guru"), [Kōbun Chino Otogawa](/wiki/K%C5%8Dbun_Chino_Otogawa "Kōbun Chino Otogawa").[292] The vegan wedding cake was in the shape of Yosemite's [Half Dome](/wiki/Half_Dome "Half Dome"), and the wedding ended with a hike and Laurene's brothers' snowball fight. Jobs reportedly said to Mona: "You see, Mona [...], Laurene is descended from [Joe Namath](/wiki/Joe_Namath "Joe Namath"), and we're descended from [John Muir](/wiki/John_Muir "John Muir")".[293]

Jobs's and Powell's first child, a son named [Reed](/wiki/Reed_Jobs "Reed Jobs"), was born in 1991.[294] Jobs's father, Paul, died a year and a half later, on March 5, 1993. Jobs's childhood home remains a tourist attraction and is currently owned by his stepmother (Paul's second wife), Marilyn Jobs.[295] Jobs and Powell had two more children, daughters Erin (b. 1995) and [Eve Jobs](/wiki/Eve_Jobs "Eve Jobs") (b. 1998), who is a fashion model.[294] The family lived in [Palo Alto, California](/wiki/Palo_Alto,_California "Palo Alto, California").[296] Although a billionaire, Jobs made it known that, like Gates, he had stipulated that most of his monetary fortune would not be left to his children.[297][298]

### Family

[Chrisann Brennan](/wiki/Chrisann_Brennan "Chrisann Brennan") notes that after Jobs was forced out of Apple, "he apologized many times over for his behavior" towards her and Lisa. She said Jobs "said that he never took responsibility when he should have, and that he was sorry".[299] By this time, Jobs had developed a strong relationship with Lisa and when she was nine, Jobs had her name on her birth certificate changed from "Lisa Brennan" to "Lisa Brennan-Jobs".[11] Jobs and Brennan developed a working relationship to [co-parent](/wiki/Co-parent "Co-parent") Lisa, a change which Brennan credits to the influence of his newly found biological sister, [Mona Simpson](/wiki/Mona_Simpson "Mona Simpson"), who worked to repair the relationship between Lisa and Jobs.[11] Jobs had found Mona after first finding his birth mother, Joanne Schieble Simpson, shortly after he left Apple.[300]

Jobs did not contact his birth family during his adoptive mother Clara's lifetime, however. He later told his official biographer [Walter Isaacson](/wiki/Walter_Isaacson "Walter Isaacson"): "I never wanted [Paul and Clara] to feel like I didn't consider them my parents, because they were totally my parents [...] I loved them so much that I never wanted them to know of my search, and I even had reporters keep it quiet when any of them found out".[300] However, in 1986, when Jobs was 31, Clara was diagnosed with lung cancer. He began to spend a great deal of time with her and learned more details about her background and his adoption, information that motivated him to find his biological mother. Jobs found on his birth certificate the name of the San Francisco doctor to whom Schieble had turned when she was pregnant. Although the doctor did not help Jobs while he was alive, he left a letter for Jobs to be opened upon his death. As he died soon afterwards, Jobs was given the letter which stated that "his mother had been an unmarried graduate student from Wisconsin named Joanne Schieble".[300]

Jobs only contacted Schieble after Clara died in early 1986 and after he received permission from his father, Paul. In addition, out of respect for Paul, he asked the media not to report on his search.[300] Jobs stated that he was motivated to find his birth mother out of both curiosity and a need "to see if she was okay and to thank her, because I'm glad I didn't end up as an abortion. She was twenty-three and she went through a lot to have me."[301] Schieble was emotional during their first meeting (though she was not familiar with the history of Apple or Jobs's role in it) and told him that she had been pressured into signing the adoption papers. She said that she regretted giving him up and repeatedly apologized to him for it. Jobs and Schieble developed a friendly relationship throughout the rest of his life and spent Christmas together.[302]

During this first visit, Schieble told Jobs that he had a sister, Mona, who was not aware that she had a brother.[301] Schieble then arranged for them to meet in New York where Mona worked. Her first impression of Jobs was that "he was totally straightforward and lovely, just a normal and sweet guy".[303] Simpson and Jobs then went for a long walk to get to know each other.[303] Jobs later told his biographer that "Mona was not completely thrilled at first to have me in her life and have her mother so emotionally affectionate toward me ... As we got to know each other, we became really good friends, and she is my family. I don't know what I'd do without her. I can't imagine a better sister. My adopted sister, Patty, and I were never close."[303]

> I grew up as an only child, with a single mother. Because we were poor and because I knew my father had emigrated from Syria, I imagined he looked like [Omar Sharif](/wiki/Omar_Sharif "Omar Sharif"). I hoped he would be rich and kind and would come into our lives (and our not-yet-furnished apartment) and help us. Later, after I'd met my father, I tried to believe he'd changed his number and left no forwarding address because he was an idealistic revolutionary, plotting a new world for the Arab people. Even as a feminist, my whole life I'd been waiting for a man to love, who could love me. For decades, I'd thought that man would be my father. When I was 25, I met that man, and he was my brother. 

— [Mona Simpson](/wiki/Mona_Simpson "Mona Simpson")[223]

Jobs then learned his family history. Six months after he was given up for adoption, Schieble's father died, she wed Jandali, and they had a daughter, Mona.[304][305] Jandali states that after finishing his PhD he returned to Syria to work, and then Schieble left him.[304] They divorced in 1962[13] and he said then he lost contact with Mona for a time: 

> I also bear the responsibility for being away from my daughter when she was four years old, as her mother divorced me when I went to Syria, but we got back in touch after 10 years. We lost touch again when her mother moved and I didn't know where she was, but since 10 years ago we've been in constant contact, and I see her three times a year. I organized a trip for her last year to visit Syria and Lebanon and she went with a relative from Florida.[304]

A few years later, Schieble married an ice-skating teacher, George Simpson. Mona Jandali took her stepfather's last name, as Mona Simpson. In 1970, after divorcing her second husband, Schieble took Mona to Los Angeles and raised her alone.[305]

When Simpson found that their father, Abdulfattah Jandali, was living in [Sacramento, California](/wiki/Sacramento,_California "Sacramento, California"), Jobs had no interest in meeting him as he believed Jandali did not treat his children well[306] and according to the _[San Francisco Chronicle](/wiki/San_Francisco_Chronicle "San Francisco Chronicle")_ , this was because of finding a _Seattle Times_ article about Jandali's abandonment of his students on a trip to Egypt in 1974.[307] Simpson went to Sacramento alone and met Jandali, who worked in a small restaurant. They spoke for several hours, and he told her that he had left teaching for the restaurant business. He said he and Schieble had given another child away for adoption but that "we'll never see that baby again. That baby's gone." He said he once managed a Mediterranean restaurant near [San Jose](/wiki/San_Jose,_California "San Jose, California") and that "all of the successful technology people used to come there. Even Steve Jobs ... oh yeah, he used to come in, and he was a sweet guy and a big tipper". At the request of Jobs, Simpson did not reveal to Jandali that his own story meant that he had actually already met his son.[308]

After hearing about the visit, Jobs recalled that "it was amazing ... I had been to that restaurant a few times, and I remember meeting the owner. He was Syrian. Balding. We shook hands." However, Jobs still did not want to meet Jandali because "I was a wealthy man by then, and I didn't trust him not to try to blackmail me or go to the press about it ... I asked Mona not to tell him about me".[308] Jandali later discovered his relationship to Jobs through an online blog. He then contacted Simpson and asked, "what is this thing about Steve Jobs?". Simpson told him that it was true and later commented, "My father is thoughtful and a beautiful storyteller, but he is very, very passive ... He never contacted Steve". Because Simpson herself researched her Syrian roots and began to meet the family, she assumed that Jobs would eventually want to meet their father, but he never did. Jobs also never showed an interest in his Syrian heritage or the Middle East. Simpson fictionalized the search for their father in her 1992 novel _[The Lost Father](/wiki/The_Lost_Father "The Lost Father")_.[302] [Malek Jandali](/wiki/Malek_Jandali "Malek Jandali") is their cousin.[309]

### Philanthropy

Jobs's views and actions on philanthropy and charity are a public mystery.[310] He maintained privacy even over what few of these actions were publicly known. He has been a key figure in public discussions about societal obligations of the wealthy and powerful. Through his career, the media investigated and criticized him and Apple as unusually and inexplicably mysterious or absent among powerful leaders and especially billionaires. His name is absent from the Million Dollar List of all large global philanthropy.[311] Some have speculated about his possible secret role in large anonymous donations.[310]

Mark Vermilion, former charitable leader for [Joan Baez](/wiki/Joan_Baez "Joan Baez"), Apple, and Jobs, attributed Jobs's lifelong minimization of direct charity to his perfectionism and limited time. Jobs, Vermilion, and supporters said over the years that corporate products were Jobs's superior contributions to culture and society instead of direct charity.[311] In 1985, Jobs said, "You know, my main reaction to this money thing is that it's humorous, all the attention to it, because it's hardly the most insightful or valuable thing that's happened to me."[310]

Shortly after leaving Apple, he formed the charitable Steven P. Jobs Foundation, led by Mark Vermilion, hired away from Apple's community leadership. Jobs wanted a focus on nutrition and vegetarianism, but Vermilion wanted social entrepreneurship. That year, Jobs soon launched NeXT and closed the foundation with no results. Upon his 1997 return to Apple, Jobs optimized the failing company to the core, such as eliminating all philanthropic programs, never to be restored. In 2007, _Stanford Social Innovation Review_ magazine listed Apple among "America's least philanthropic companies". A few months after another unflattering news report, Apple started a program to match employees' charitable gifts.[311] Jobs declined to sign [The Giving Pledge](/wiki/The_Giving_Pledge "The Giving Pledge"), launched in 2010 by [Warren Buffett](/wiki/Warren_Buffett "Warren Buffett") and [Bill Gates](/wiki/Bill_Gates "Bill Gates") for fellow billionaires.[311][310] He donated $50 million to Stanford hospital and contributed to efforts to cure AIDS. [Bono](/wiki/Bono "Bono") reported "tens of millions of dollars" given by Apple while Jobs was CEO, to AIDS and HIV relief programs in Africa, which inspired other companies to join.[311]

## Honors and awards

[](/wiki/File:President_Ronald_Reagan_presents_the_1985_National_Technology_Awards_to_Steven_Jobs.jpg)Jobs received the [National Medal of Technology](/wiki/National_Medal_of_Technology "National Medal of Technology") from President [Ronald Reagan](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan "Ronald Reagan") in 1985, awarded jointly with [Steve Wozniak](/wiki/Steve_Wozniak "Steve Wozniak"). [](/wiki/File:Steve_Jobs_\(1\).JPG)A statue of Jobs at Graphisoft Park in [Budapest](/wiki/Budapest "Budapest")[312]

  * **1985** : awarded [National Medal of Technology](/wiki/National_Medal_of_Technology "National Medal of Technology") (with [Steve Wozniak](/wiki/Steve_Wozniak "Steve Wozniak")) by US president [Ronald Reagan](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan "Ronald Reagan"), the country's highest honor for technological achievements[313]
  * **1987** : [Jefferson Award for Public Service](/wiki/Jefferson_Award_for_Public_Service "Jefferson Award for Public Service")[314]
  * **1989** : _Entrepreneur of the Decade_ by _[Inc.](/wiki/Inc._\(magazine\) "Inc. \(magazine\)")_[315]
  * **1991** : [Howard Vollum Award](/wiki/Howard_Vollum_Award "Howard Vollum Award") from [Reed College](/wiki/Reed_College "Reed College")[316]
  * **2004–2010** : listed among the [_Time_ 100 Most Influential People in the World](/wiki/Time_100 "Time 100") on five separate occasions[317]
  * **2007** : named the most powerful person in business by _[Fortune](/wiki/Fortune_\(magazine\) "Fortune \(magazine\)")_ magazine[318]
  * **2007** : inducted into the [California Hall of Fame](/wiki/California_Hall_of_Fame "California Hall of Fame"), located at [The California Museum for History, Women and the Arts](/wiki/The_California_Museum_for_History,_Women_and_the_Arts "The California Museum for History, Women and the Arts")[319]
  * **2012** : [Grammy Trustees Award](/wiki/Grammy_Trustees_Award "Grammy Trustees Award"), an award for those who have influenced the music industry in areas unrelated to performance[320]
  * **2012** : posthumously honored with an [Edison Achievement Award](/wiki/Edison_Achievement_Award "Edison Achievement Award") for his commitment to innovation throughout his career[321]
  * **2013** : posthumously inducted as a [Disney Legend](/wiki/Disney_Legend "Disney Legend")[322]
  * **2017** : [Steve Jobs Theater](/wiki/Steve_Jobs_Theater "Steve Jobs Theater") opens at [Apple Park](/wiki/Apple_Park "Apple Park")[323]
  * **2022** : posthumously awarded the [Presidential Medal of Freedom](/wiki/Presidential_Medal_of_Freedom "Presidential Medal of Freedom") by US president [Joe Biden](/wiki/Joe_Biden "Joe Biden"), the country's highest civilian honor[324]
